市場焦點
美國
• Fed褐皮書顯示，美國經濟整體維持溫和擴張，10個地區經濟活動小幅至溫和成長，消費及企
業需求仍有支撐 ，但住宅市場偏弱 、消費者價格敏感度升高 。就業僅小幅增加 ，薪資溫和成
長；另一方面 ，能源、運輸、原物料及關稅成本持續推升企業支出 ，物價在多數地區溫和上
升，反映經濟韌性與通膨壓力並存，增加Fed政策判斷難度。
美伊衝突
• 美國８月底宣布對逾 60個伊朗相關實體實施制裁 ，試圖切斷伊朗的石油 、金融與貿易收入 。
但伊朗歷經數十年制裁後 ，已建立相對成熟的制裁規避網絡 ，但短期預期美國仍難迫使德黑
蘭屈服。對金融市場而言 ，若制裁未進一步衝擊伊朗石油出口 ，油價上行空間相對有限；反
之，若美國擴大次級制裁並波及中國等主要買家，則可能推升原油價格與全球通膨預期。

韓股
• 儘管市場波動劇烈 ，韓國企業在第⼆季度財報季的盈利前景仍在持續改善 ，在三星電⼦和 SK
海力⼠公佈業績後，MSCI韓國指數的全年每股純益預期被小幅上調 ，此前5至6月修正步伐一
度放緩，如今⼜重新加快，整體來看，今年韓國盈利預期累計上調幅度接近 190%，並推動整
個新興市場盈利預期提升約 37%，晶⽚需求指引依然強勁 ，隨著盈利預測持續上調 ，台灣市
場也從中受益。

美國製造業維持擴張，需求降溫與高成本壓力牽制擴張動能
• 新訂單降溫，製造業維持擴張韌性 ：美國8月ISM製造業PMI降至54.6%，較7月55.6%回落，但仍連續第8個月位於擴張
區間；新訂單降至 53.7%、訂單積壓降至 51.8%，顯示需求動能較前月放緩 ，惟生產指數仍達 58.3%，連續第10個月擴
張，運輸設備、機械及石油與煤炭等主要產業維持成長，製造業整體仍具韌性。
• 生產韌性延續，價格壓力仍偏高：就業指數降至51.2%，雖較前月走弱但仍處擴張區間；供應商交貨指數升至59.3%，代
表供應商交期進一步拉長 ，而價格指數維持71.1%，原物料價格已連續 23個月上升，鋼鋁、關稅及石化產品成本仍是主
要壓力來源。整體呈現「需求降溫、生產偏強、成本仍高」的格局，企業利潤與通膨傳導仍需觀察。

• 後市觀察：製造業景氣仍具韌性 ，但新訂單放緩與投入成本高檔並存 ，後續應觀察需求能否延續 ，以及鋼鋁、能源與關
稅成本是否進一步轉嫁至終端價格上 。投資布局可聚焦半導體 、電機電⼦及AI基礎建設等受惠於製造投資與 AI資本支出
的領域；債券則以中短天期及投資等級公司債為主，降低利率波動風險，以股債配置分散景氣與利率風險。
ISM製造業PMI關鍵分項指標比較
資料來源：ISM Manufacturing PMI Report ，2026.9.1
ISM製造業PMI月變動趨勢

褐皮書揭美國經濟溫和成長，成本壓力增添Fed政策難度
• 經濟溫和擴張，消費與企業需求仍有支撐：Fed褐皮書
顯示，自7月初至8月下旬，10個地區經濟活動小幅至
溫和成長、2個地區持平，消費支出整體小幅增加 ，製
造業、部分服務業及金融活動有所改善 ，資料中心與
國防相關需求亦提供支撐；但住宅市場表現仍偏弱 ，
消費者對價格更加敏感 ，顯示經濟維持韌性但內需表
現分化。
• 就業增幅有限 ，成本壓力仍牽動通膨 ：就業整體僅小
幅增加，7個地區就業成長 、5個地區大致持平 ，薪資
多數維持溫和上升；然而能源 、運輸、金屬、石化原
料及關稅成本仍持續推升企業投入成本 ，部分企業甚
至難以完全轉嫁成本，整體價格仍呈現上升。
• 市場觀察： 褐皮書反映美國經濟尚未明顯轉弱 ，但就

業降溫與通膨壓力並存，使Fed政策取向更具挑戰。近
期市場對9月升息的預期明顯升溫 ，政策仍高度依賴後
續通膨與就業數據 。建議債券配置宜以短中天期美債
及投資級公司債為主 ，降低長天期利率波動；股票則
聚焦大型優質科技 、AI基礎建設及高現金流企業 ，並
搭配黃金分散通膨與地緣政治風險。
資料來源：Fed Beige Book，兆豐銀行整理， 2026.9.3

制裁使伊朗經濟受創，仍難迫使政權屈服
上海合作組織(SCO)佔全球經濟(GDP)的四分之一
資料來源：IMF，Bloomberg，2026.9.1，其他上海合作組織成員國包括巴基斯坦、伊朗、哈薩克、烏茲別克、
白俄羅斯、蒙古、吉爾吉斯、塔吉克和阿富汗資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.8.26
伊朗絕大部分石油銷往中國
• 制裁聲勢強，但關鍵仍在中國：美國８月底宣布對逾60個伊朗相關實體實施制裁，試圖切斷伊朗的石油、金融與貿易收入。
制裁能否真正奏效，關鍵仍在於是否將中國納入主要打擊對象。伊朗近八成的石油銷往中國，美方現階段僅將少數中港企業
列入黑名單，刻意避開了中國大型國有金融與能源巨頭，若強行實施全面制裁，可能危及關鍵礦產供應、破壞脆弱的美中貿
易休兵，甚至推升全球能源價格。

• 制裁使伊朗經濟受創，仍難迫使政權屈服：伊朗歷經數十年制裁後，已建立相對成熟的制裁規避網絡，並透過中國、俄羅斯、
印度及中亞國家參與的上海合作組織（SCO）等非西方平台維持經貿往來。俄羅斯與伊朗合作持續深化，巴基斯坦亦表態無
義務遵守美方制裁，印度則在制裁壓力下維持一定程度的經貿交流。因此，美國新一輪制裁雖將加劇伊朗經濟壓力，但短期
仍難迫使德黑蘭屈服。對金融市場而言，若制裁未進一步衝擊伊朗石油出口，油價上行空間相對有限；反之，若美國擴大次
級制裁並波及中國等主要買家，則可能推升原油價格與全球通膨預期，進而延後聯準會降息時程，增加股債市場波動。

歐元區通膨率升至近三年新高，９月升息機率高
• 歐洲央行9月升息機率高：歐洲央行（ECB）9月升息至2.50%的預期已逐步升溫，主因歐元區8月通膨升至3.3%，加上能源
價格受伊朗戰事推升，強化鷹派對進一步緊縮的主張。不過，核心通膨降至2.4%、服務通膨趨緩，且長期通膨預期仍大致穩
定，顯示能源價格尚未明顯向薪資與核心物價擴散，因此，9月升息後暫停仍是較可能的政策路徑。 9月升息後，ECB是否於
12月再次升息，將取決於能源衝擊的持續時間及其向核心通膨的傳導程度。
• 12月是否再升息，能源價格是關鍵：若伊朗戰事延續、油氣價格維持高檔，鷹派可能推動利率於12月進一步升至2.75%；反
之，若能源價格回落、服務通膨持續降溫，則本輪升息可能在9月告終。整體而言，ECB政策已轉向偏鷹，但在缺乏能源衝

擊廣泛傳導至核心通膨的明確證據下，年內連續升息空間有限。在政策利率逐步觸頂的情況下，歐債殖利率若因升息預期而
上行，反而可提供較佳的中長期配置與收益機會。
歐元區通膨（CPI） ECBspeak指數暗示歐洲央行還會進一步加息
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.2 資料來源：，2026.902

韓企業績展望在第⼆季財報加速改善
• 韓企業績展望在第⼆季財報加速改善 ：儘管市場波動劇烈 ，韓國企業在第⼆季財報的 獲利前景仍在持續改善 ，在三星電⼦和
SK海力⼠公佈業績後，MSCI韓國指數的全年每股純益預期被小幅上調 ，此前5至6月修正步伐一度放緩，如今⼜重新加快，整
體來看今年韓國獲利預期累計上調幅度接近 190%，並推動整個新興市場 獲利預期提升約37%，晶⽚需求指引依然強勁 ，隨著
獲利預測持續上調，台灣市場也從中受益。
• 展望後市：在韓國政府推動Value-up政策下，企業愈來愈重視股東權益 ，透過提高現金股利及擴大 買回庫藏股回饋股東，有
望成為第三季韓股上漲的重要動能，尤其是記憶體產業龍頭企業，擁有充沛的自由現金流，實施庫藏股買回，不僅能提升每股
價值，也反映管理層對未來獲利前景具信心，並認為目前股價被低估，參考日本推動公司治理改革後帶動股市重評價的經驗 ，

預期韓股後市仍具支撐力，若市場出現短期回檔，反而可視為逢低布局的機會。

阿里巴巴AI營收佳， 2027財年資本支出料將創下歷史新高

期戰略方向，相關受惠企業可望持續成長 ，另一方面科網龍頭的AI業務已逐步走出高投入階段，開始展現商業化成果與獲利

美債市場結構性轉變：從不挑剔的機構到追求回報的私人投資者
• 自新冠疫情以來 ，美國10年期公債殖利率持續走高 ，過去市場多半歸咎於聯準會升息或通膨 ，但彭博最新研究顯示 ，期限
溢價的顯著上升才是主要推手，其背後的根本原因是美債買家發生了結構性轉變。
• 傳統非價格敏感型買家縮手：過去幾十年，聯準會(透過QE)與外國央行是美債的兩大底氣來源，他們買美債是為了儲備與流
動性，對價格不敏感，因此能有效壓低利率與期限溢價 。自2020年以來，聯準會與外國官方持有的美債佔美國 GDP比重分
別下降12%與8%，光是這兩大買家的退場，就推升10年期美債期限溢價約1.05%。
• 私人投資者接棒，市場對殖利率極度挑剔： 目前的邊際買家已轉為外國私人投資者 (如基金、保險公司等)，私人買家會精算

美債、公司債、股票與各國主權債的收益差異，進行嚴格比價。當美債發行量增加時，必須給予更高的殖利率才能吸引他們
購買，這使得美債利率波動更加劇烈。
自疫情以來，長期利率呈上升趨勢
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.1
主要機構買家持有的美國國債減少

供給端新挑戰：AI浪潮帶動公司債暴增，搶奪資金池
• 科技巨頭發債融資潮 ，公司債成為美債的強勁替代品： 受人工智慧建設資本支出帶動 ，大型科技企業 (如Alphabet、Meta、
Amazon等)積極發行投資級公司債 。這些高品質科技公司債提供優異回報 ，吸引了大量私人資金從美債轉向公司債 。為了
與這些高收益的高評級公司債競爭資金，美國國債殖利率面臨更大的上行壓力。
• 小結及建議：當前美債殖利率居高不下，核心原因在於穩定的買家減少，而接手的私人買家變得挑剔，加上AI熱潮創造了高
回報的公司債競爭源。在美國財政部發債壓力未減的情況下，長端殖利率短期內難大幅下行。債券策略建議採取配置中短天
期債券，長天期公債(10年期以上)價格受期限溢價與供給壓力壓制，波動較大，建議縮短持債年期(如布局3-5年期)，鎖定高

殖利率的同時降低價格波動風險 ，加碼高品質投資級公司債，目前私人資金偏好高評級公司債(尤其是科技與AI龍頭債)，這
類債券信用風險極低，收益率卻優於同天期公債，是當前固定收益資產的優質選擇。
外國私人投資者購買美國公債和公司債
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.1
人工智慧相關債務發行只是殖利率上升的原因之一

日圓再現匯率干預跡象 套利交易平倉恐加劇日圓升值
• 日圓再現匯率干預跡象：上週週間，日圓兌美元匯價出現單日大幅升值情況，市場再度懷疑官方出手干預匯率。然而，彭博
指出，日本央行帳戶數據沒有大規模買入日元跡象，意味著匯率的劇烈波動可能來自市場重新調整日本央行加息預期，而非
日本政府再次入市干預。與此同時，美國財政部並未回應是否參與干預。
• 市場懷疑干預之際，套利交易平倉可能正在發生：儘管無法證實官方上週是否直接干預日圓匯價，但日圓走升可能也反映上
週日本央行官員高田創釋出鷹派升息的態度，將不排除一次性大幅升息或連續升息的可能性。根據芝加哥商品交易所的數據
顯示，日圓融資的套利交易正在平倉，而且中期內持有日圓而非其他G10貨幣的意願非常強烈。
• 觀點：2024年8月日本央行意外升息，引發日圓套利交易平倉，全球主要國家股市短期間出現修正，當時AI與半導體成為套

利資金撤離重點。本次匯價波動暫時可歸咎於套利交易倉位的調整，尚未出現恐慌賣壓，重點將關注在本月中的日本央行利
率會議，留意意外大幅升息的風險。
資料來源：Bloomberg，截至2026.9.3。

• 今年年初黃金價格創下歷史新高，突破每盎司5,000美元大關，成為金融市場最受關注的資產之一，隨後經歷幾個月的高檔
修正後，上個月再度獲得市場資金追捧，現貨黃金價格單月大漲近10%。過去市場認為黃金上漲主要來自地緣政治風險與央
行降息預期，但近期投資邏輯似乎正在改變。
• 黃金多頭始自央行「去美元化」：本輪黃金多頭始自2022年烏俄衝突，西方國家凍結俄羅斯央行的大部分海外美元與歐元
外匯儲備，讓各國央行開始意識到外匯儲備的安全性，進而推動外匯儲備多元化。透過適度減持美元(美債)來佈局實體黃金
或其他國家貨幣，被成為「去美元化」趨勢。
• 「貶值交易」有望接棒黃金上漲動能：上個月美國公布最新國債規模已突破40兆美元，市場開始擔憂財政赤字的可維持性，
貨幣信心下降導致「貶值交易」發生(美債信心下降)，除了導致長天期美債殖利率上揚之外，貨幣避險需求再度讓黃金資產

成為市場焦點，8月份現貨黃金一度上漲超過15%，隨後雖因聯準會釋出鷹派訊號，單月漲幅仍接近10%。
貶值交易趨勢下，黃金的投資機會
全球央行黃金儲備佔比
資料來源：世界黃金協會、富蘭克林投顧，截至2025年，兆豐銀行整理。

• 央行買盤仍為黃金中期表現帶來利多：根據世界黃金協會2026年各國央行調查，上半年截至5/19，有45%的央行預期未來
一年將增加自身黃金儲備，創調查9年來新高。央行增持黃金主要考量包含：儲備多元化、美元走弱預期、地緣政治衝突以
及金價長期上漲趨勢，顯示黃金已成為各國儲備資產的重要戰略配置，在強勁的需求買盤下，多數機構預測黃金將維持多頭
至明年年底。
• 觀點：近期黃金價格反彈後，可能會受到獲利了結以及利率政策預期變化而出現波動，但在全球債務問題、貨幣貶值風險及
央行買盤支撐下，在中期內仍有機會走出慢牛行情。建議投資人以資產配置的角度，逢修正適度配置黃金資產(如：黃金ETF)。
風險接受度較高的投資人，則可關注金礦類股，但需注意其對於美元匯、利率的敏感度較高，資產價格波動較大。

前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 本資料所載內容如涉及商品資訊 ，商品相關資訊仍以經核可之條款及文宣 (DM)為主，本行網站已備有公開說明書可供查閱基金應負擔之費用及相關投資風險 。投
資人申購基金係持有基金受益憑證，而非本資料提及之投資資產或標的。
5. 本資料所載內容取自本公司相信可靠之資料來源，且為特定日期之判斷，本公司不保證其即時性、精確性及完整性，亦不提供或嘗試遊說客戶做為投資之判斷依據。

資料內容邇後若有變更，本公司將不做預告或主動更新。投資人於決策時應審慎衡量本身風險，如因信賴本資料內容而做出或改變投資決策，須自行承擔結果，本
公司及本公司所屬員工，均不負任何損害賠償及其他法律上之責任。
6. 本資料內容之著作權屬兆豐銀行所有，禁止就所載資料任何形式之抄襲、引用或轉載。
7. 本資料所載部分基金涉及非投資等級債，投資人投資以非投資等級債券為訴求之基金不宜占其投資組合過高之比重 。基金經金管會核准，惟不表示絕無風險。由於
非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。
11. 屬基金資訊觀測站ESG基金專區所列之基金，有關基金之ESG資訊，投資人應於申購前詳閱基金公開說明書或投資人須知所載之基金所有特色或目標等資訊。
警 語

---
## Document 038: 2027年春闘のスケジュールと金融政策 ～2027年春闘の行方は3月より前に見えてくる～

Source PDF: all report pdf/2027年春闘のスケジュールと金融政策 ～2027年春闘の行方は3月より前に見えてくる～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Economic Trends

2026 年 9 月 17 日

1/8 
2027 年春闘のスケジュールと金融政策 
～2027 年春闘の行方は 3 月より前に見えてくる～ 
 経済調査部 シニアエグゼクティブエコノミスト  新家 義貴 
（℡：050-5474-7490）

（要旨） 
○ 2027 年春闘に向けて、2026 年10 月の連合 「春季生活闘争基本構想」 を皮切りに、 経団連 「経
労委報告」原案、主要産別の要求方針、早期賃上げ表明企業など、重要イベントが秋から冬
にかけて相次ぐ。３月の集中回答日を待たず、春闘の「初動のモメンタム」を段階的に把握
することが可能である。 
○ 2027 年春闘では、2026 年まで３年連続で続いた高い賃上げが維持されるかが焦点となる。 主
要産業別労働組合の要求水準や早期賃上げ表明企業の広がり、中小企業への波及などを通じ
て、賃上げの持続性と広がりを見極める必要がある。 
○ 足元では物価上振れリスクが日銀の金融政策判断の中心にあるが、賃金上昇の持続性も引き
続き重要である。 春闘関連情報は26 年 10 月時点では限定的だが、12 月には労使双方の姿勢
や具体的な要求水準がかなり明らかになり、１月にはさらに幅広い企業情報を確認できる。
2025 年の経験を踏まえれば、年内に確認される「初動のモメンタム」が利上げ判断を後押し
する一つの材料になる可能性がある。

１．2027 年春闘に向けたスケジュールを整理する 
2027 年春闘への注目が早くも高まりつつある。連合の最終集計によると、2026 年春闘の賃上げ
率は 5.01％となり、前年の5.25％をやや下回ったものの、３年連続で５％台の高い賃上げとなっ
た（厚生労働省「民間主要企業春季賃上げ要求・妥結状況」ベースでは5.18％）。2027 年春闘で
は、こうした高い賃上げが４年目も続くかが最大の焦点となる。 
春闘の方向性を判断するための材料は、３月の集中回答日を待たず、秋以降に順次明らかにな
る。連合の基本構想、経団連の経労委報告原案、産別の要求方針、早期賃上げ表明などを通じ
て、春闘の「初動のモメンタム」を段階的に確認することができる。 
こうした情報は金融政策を考えるうえでも無視できない。足元では物価上振れリスクが日銀の
利上げ判断の中心にあるが、賃金上昇の持続性は引き続き重要な判断材料である。本稿では、
2027 年春闘の行方を見極めるうえで押さえておくべき主要イベントや関連経済指標を時系列で整
理するとともに、金融政策との関係について考える。

### Page 2

2/8

連合「春季生活闘争基本構想」 （10 月中～下旬） 
春闘関連で最初に注目されるイベントが、連合が毎年10 月中～下旬に公表する「春季生活闘争
基本構想」である（26 年春闘では25 年 10 月23 日公表） 。翌年の春闘に向けての労働組合側の基
本理念や方針、重点課題を示すものであり、経済状況や社会課題を踏まえ、賃上げ目標や格差是
正、適正取引の促進などを掲げる。なかでも注目されるのが賃上げの目安であり、昨年はベース
アップを「３％以上」 、定期昇給分を含めた総額で「５％以上」 、中小企業は大手との格差是正を
図るため「６％以上」の賃上げを求める方針が示されていた。前年の基本構想と比較すること
で、組合側が翌年の春闘に向けてより強気か慎重かを判断することができ、春闘全体のトーンを
つかむうえで重要な材料となる。 
なお、この「春季生活闘争基本構想」をたたき台とした「春季生活闘争方針」が毎年11 月末～
12 月初に公表されるが、賃上げの目安については通常、大きな変更はない。

経団連「経労委報告」の原案判明（11 月上～中旬） 
経労委報告（経営労働政策特別委員会報告）は、経団連が毎年冬に公表する、春闘に対する経
営側の基本スタンスを示す報告書である。経営側の雇用政策に関する方向性や賃上げの考え方な
ど、春闘交渉に向けた経営側の指針となる。正式な報告書は毎年１月下旬に公表されるが、その
原案については前年の11 月上旬頃に判明し、報道されることが多い。経営側が翌年の春闘にどの
ような姿勢で臨もうとしているのかを早い段階で把握できるため、注目度は高い。 
2027 年春闘に向けては、高い賃上げを４年目も維持する姿勢が示されるかが焦点となる。人手
不足や採用競争の激化、物価上昇の持続は引き続き賃上げを促す一方、数年にわたる賃上げで人
件費負担は累積しており、イラン情勢を背景とした原材料費や物流費の上昇も企業収益を圧迫す
る可能性がある。こうした環境下でも賃上げ継続に前向きな姿勢を維持するのか、それとも収益
環境への配慮から慎重なトーンを強めるのかが注目される。また、中小企業への賃上げ波及や価
格転嫁、取引適正化をどのように位置づけるかも重要なポイントとなろう。 
なお、現時点で経営側から2027 年春闘に向けた初期的なメッセージが出始めている。経団連の
筒井会長は9 月7 日の記者会見で、 「ベースアップ実施の検討を引き続きスタンダードと位置付け
ていきたい」と述べ、賃金引き上げの力強いモメンタムを維持したいとの考えを示している。イ
ラン情勢悪化の長期化と原油高は不安要素ではあるものの、現時点では、経営側が賃上げ姿勢を
後退させている様子はみられない。

政労使会議（11 月下旬頃） 
政労使会議は、政府、労働側（連合など） 、経営側（経団連など）の代表が、春闘の賃上げをは
じめとする労働条件の改善について意見交換を行う場である。政府から労使に賃上げを要請する
ことが多く、賃上げに向けた社会的な機運を確認するうえで注目される。2026 年春闘に向けては
2025 年11 月25 日に開催され、高市首相が「一昨年、昨年の水準と遜色のない水準での賃上げ」

### Page 3

3/8 
と「物価上昇に負けないベースアップ」を要請したほか、経営側、労働側も高い賃上げの継続に
前向きな姿勢を示した。 
2027 年春闘に向けても、政府がどの程度強く賃上げを求めるのかに加え、経営側、労働側が高
い賃上げの継続についてどの程度認識を共有しているかが焦点となる。また、中小企業の価格転
嫁や取引適正化、賃上げ原資の確保に向けた支援策がどの程度打ち出されるかも重要である。政
府が企業のコスト負担を和らげつつ、賃上げを後押しするためにどのような環境整備を打ち出す
かにも注目したい。

民間シンクタンクの春闘賃上げ率予測（11 月～12 月） 
 毎年 11 月～12 月にかけて、民間シンクタンク・調査機関から翌年の春闘賃上げ率の予測が出さ
れることが多い（筆者は11 月に作成している） 。予測をレポートとして作成することもあれば、
経済見通しレポートのなかで言及されることもある。予測の精度についてはさておき、翌年の春
闘賃上げ率についての相場観は、この時期に徐々に形成される。 
2027 年春闘予測で注目されるのは、前年並み、あるいはそれ以上の高い賃上げを予想するの
か、それとも一定の鈍化を見込むのかである。後者の場合でも、 「やや鈍化するが高水準」と高い
賃上げが持続するとの評価か、それともある程度の鈍化を見込んで「５％割れ」を強調するか
で、春闘への評価に違いが出る。2026 年春闘賃上げ率は連合ベースで5.01％、厚生労働省ベース
で 5.18％であり、2027 年春闘では、５％前後の高い賃上げを維持できるかが一つの焦点となる。 
2027 年春闘では、構造的な人手不足、物価高、高水準の企業業績は賃金の押し上げ要因になる
一方、数年にわたる人件費負担の累積や、原材料費・物流費の上昇は賃上げ抑制要因となる。各
機関の予測を比較することで、2027 年春闘に対する現時点での相場観や、賃上げを支える力がど
の程度続くとみられているのかを把握することができるだろう。

産業別労働組合組織の賃上げ要求案判明（11 月～） 
11 月に入ると、主要な産業別労働組合組織で翌年春闘に向けた賃上げ要求方針案が報道等を通
じて明らかになり始める。正式な要求方針は１月～２月にかけて発表されるが、正式決定前に報
道等で概要が先行して判明するのがこの時期である。前年との比較で要求水準が引き上げられる
のか、維持されるのか、あるいは抑制されるのかを早い段階で具体的な数字として確認できるた
め、春闘全体の相場観を占ううえで重要な材料となる。 
前回の2026 年春闘では、11 月初めからＵＡゼンセンなどの要求方針案が明らかになり、その
後、基幹労連、ＪＡＭ、全電線、私鉄総連、自動車総連、電機連合などでも、前年並みか前年を
やや上回る要求方針の報道が相次いだ。こうした産別の要求方針が、2026 年春闘の「初動のモメ
ンタム」を判断する重要な材料となった。 
2027 年春闘でも、主要産別が前年並みの高い要求を維持するのか、それとも要求水準に変化が
みられるのかが注目される。特に、自動車や電機など春闘相場への影響が大きい産別の要求は注
目度が高い。

### Page 4

4/8

早期賃上げ表明企業の登場（12 月～１月） 
近年では、春闘の本格交渉を待たず、年末から年始にかけて翌年度の賃上げ方針を公表する企
業も増えている。 
賃上げに積極的な企業であることをアピールする目的もあってか、早期表明を行う企業は大幅
賃上げを行うことが多い。そのため、こうした企業が多ければ賃上げムードが醸成される効果が
ある。また、賃上げ表明を行った企業のライバル企業に賃上げ圧力を及ぼす面もあるだろう。  
特に今回は、コスト上昇が続くなかでも大幅な賃上げを早期に表明する企業が相次ぐかどうか
が重要である。こうした企業が多ければ、企業の賃上げ余力や人材確保意欲がなお強いことを示
す材料となる。一方、前年と比べて早期表明の動きが弱ければ、2027 年春闘に対する慎重な見方
が広がる可能性がある。

経済三団体共催・新年祝賀会（1 月上旬） 
 年明けには、経団連、経済同友会、日本商工会議所による新年祝賀会が開催される。各団体ト
ップや大手企業経営者から春闘についてコメントが出ることが多く、年明け時点での経営側の賃
上げ姿勢を確認する機会となる。 
特に、年末までに示された経労委報告原案や早期賃上げ表明のトーンが維持されているかを確
認する意味で重要である。また、秋から冬にかけての経済環境の変化を受け、経営側の姿勢に変
化が出ていないかも確認したい。

経団連と連合の懇談会（１月下旬） 
 経団連と連合のトップが春闘や社会課題について意見交換を行う場である。これをもって春闘
の事実上のスタートと言われるイベントである。

組合による賃上げ要求提出（２月頃） 
産業別組合組織の要求方針等を受けて、個別の組合が詳細を検討し、要求水準を固める。２月
中旬から下旬にかけて会社側に提出されることが多いが、高い賃上げを要求する組合があった場
合、大きく報道されることがある。

連合・春闘要求集計結果（３月上旬）  
３月上旬に、連合が加盟組合の賃上げ要求を集計した結果を公表する（前回は2026 年３月５日
公表） 。要求と実際の賃上げの連動性は非常に高いため、この段階になると2027 年春闘の最終結
果についてかなり精度の高い見通しが可能になる。 
また、この調査では、組合員300 人以上の組合と300 人未満の中小組合に分けて集計している
ことから、中小組合の賃上げ要求動向を把握できる点も重要である。大企業だけでなく、中小組
合にも高い賃上げ要求が広がっているかどうかを確認できる。

### Page 5

5/8

春闘の集中回答日（３月中旬） 
春闘の集中回答日とは、主要企業における労使交渉の回答が一斉に行われる日のことで、通
常、３月中旬に設定される（前回は2026 年３月18 日） 。労働組合は、交渉力を強めるために同じ
業界の労働組合同士で連携するが、その際、交渉日程でも歩調を合わせることから、回答日が集
中する。 
なお、中小企業の交渉は大企業よりも遅いタイミングで行われることが多いが、その際には集
中回答日で形成された賃上げ相場が一つの目安となる。このため、中小企業への賃上げ波及とい
う観点からも重要である。集中回答日では、近年は満額回答が相次いできたが、2027 年もこうし
た流れが続くかが注目される。

連合・第１回回答集計公表（３月中旬） 
集中回答日から数日後に、連合から春闘の第１回回答集計が公表される（前回は2026 年３月23
日） 。この春闘回答集計は、第１回集計から７月上旬に公表される最終集計まで計７回の調査があ
り、集計が進むにつれて多少下振れていくことが多いが、修正幅は例年それほど大きくない。そ
のため、この結果によって、その年の春闘賃上げ率の大勢がほぼ決することになる。注目度は非
常に高く、大きく報道されることが多い。なお、春闘で決まった賃上げは、５月以降に反映が本
格化し、その後も７～８月頃まで徐々に反映が進んでいく傾向がある。

2．春闘の解像度は段階的に高まる 
ここまで挙げた春闘関連イベントはいずれも重要であるが、なかでも注目度が高いのは、①経
団連「経労委報告」の原案判明、②産業別労働組合組織の賃上げ要求案、③早期賃上げ表明企業
の登場、④連合・春闘要求集計結果、⑤集中回答日、⑥連合・第１回回答集計である。①で経営
側の基本スタンスを確認し、②で具体的な要求水準を把握、③で企業側の賃上げ意欲や賃上げム
ードの広がりを確認する。その後、④～⑥で要求・回答の具体的な数字が明らかになり、春闘の
全体像が固まっていく。 
このように、時間の経過とともに2027 年春闘の解像度は徐々に高まる。もちろん、第１回回答
集計まで待てば賃上げ率の大勢はほぼ判明するが、それ以前でも、経営側の姿勢や産別の要求水
準、早期賃上げ表明企業の動向などを積み上げることで、高い賃上げが続く可能性をある程度判
断することは可能である。

3．2027 年春闘を読む上で併せて注目したい材料 
ここまで挙げた春闘関連イベントに加え、2027 年春闘の行方を考える上で、賃上げを取り巻く
経済環境も併せて確認する必要がある。なかでも注目したいのが、日銀短観、日銀支店長会議、
消費者物価である。これらは春闘そのもののイベントではないが、企業の賃上げ余力や賃上げ姿
勢、労働側の要求水準を左右する重要な材料となる。

### Page 6

6/8

日銀短観（26 年 10 月１日、12 月 14 日、27 年４月１日） 
日銀短観はこの先、10 月１日（９月調査） 、12 月 14 日（12 月調査） 、４月１日（３月調査）に
公表される。日銀は景気指標として日銀短観を最重要視しており、金融政策への影響も大きい。
イラン情勢が企業業績や企業景況感にどの程度悪影響を及ぼしているか（いないか） 、設備投資計
画に下振れはみられないか、物価上振れの可能性はいかほどかといったことを確認していくこと
になる。26 年度の企業業績が堅調さを保っているのであれば、27 年春闘でも高い賃上げが実現す
る可能性が高まる。逆に、業績下振れが生じれば、春闘への慎重論につながる可能性がある。

日銀支店長会議（26 年 10 月８日、27 年１月、４月） 
 日銀支店長会議は日本銀行の正副総裁や審議委員、各地の支店長が参加し、年４回開催され
る。各支店長は自分の担当する地域の金融機関や企業から集めた生の声に基づき、その地域の経
済・金融情勢を報告する。各地域の景気・産業動向や企業の設備投資・賃上げ意欲など、企業ヒ
アリングを通じて、大企業だけでなく中小企業の賃上げ姿勢や価格転嫁の状況、人手不足感など
を把握できる。前述の春闘関連イベントでは、早期に把握できるものの大半は大企業に関する情
報であり、雇用者の大半を占める中小企業の動向については把握が難しい。そのため、中小企業
の動向を確認できるという点で、日銀支店長会議の重要性は高い。 
2027 年春闘に向けては、2026 年10 月８日、2027 年１月の支店長会議が注目される。10 月で
は、2027 年春闘に向けた企業の初期的な賃上げ姿勢がどの程度みられるかが焦点となる。さらに
１月になると、春闘を間近に控え、企業側の考えもかなり固まってくる。年末までに判明した経
労委報告原案や産別の要求方針、早期賃上げ表明企業などの情報と合わせることで、春闘の方向
性をかなり具体的に把握できるようになるだろう。コスト上昇が地方企業や中小企業にどの程度
影響しているか、価格転嫁が進んでいるか、賃上げ姿勢が維持されているかといった「統計では
捉えにくい情報」を確認できる点で注目度は高い。

消費者物価指数 
物価動向も2027 年春闘の要求水準を左右する重要な材料である。賃上げ交渉では、足元の物価
上昇率や生活コストの上昇が強く意識されるためだ。特に2026 年秋から2027 年初にかけての物
価動向が、労働側の要求姿勢に影響を与える可能性が高い。 
特に注目したいのは、食料品や日用品、サービス価格の動きである。イラン情勢悪化後に生じ
た原材料費、包装資材費、物流費などの上昇が、どの程度消費者価格へ波及するかがポイントと
なる。物価上昇が高めに推移すれば、労働側にとって実質賃金の改善を求める根拠となり、高い
賃上げ要求を後押しする可能性がある。 
また、ＣＰＩ全体の上昇率だけでなく、その中身にも注目したい。仮に政府の支援策によって
エネルギー価格が一時的に押し下げられる場合でも、基調的な価格上昇が続けば、家計の生活実
感としての物価高は残りやすい。こうした物価動向が、2027 年春闘における労働側の要求水準

### Page 7

7/8 
や、実質賃金改善への意識にどの程度反映されるかを確認していく必要がある。

4．金融政策への影響をどうみるか  
2027 年春闘に関する情報は、秋以降、段階的に積み上がっていく。この点は、今後の金融政策
を考えるうえでも重要である。足元の日銀は、基調的な物価上昇率が２％に近づくなかで、原油
高や価格転嫁の広がりなどを通じた物価上振れリスクを強く意識しており、春闘を利上げ判断の
中心に据えているわけではない。 
一方、賃金上昇が持続し、それがサービス価格などへ波及していくかどうかは、基調的な物価
動向を判断するうえで引き続き重要である。春闘は現時点では金融政策判断の中心的な材料では
ないものの、秋から冬にかけて春闘の解像度が高まるにつれて、その位置づけが徐々に高まる可
能性がある。 
 こうした観点からみると、秋から冬にかけて春闘関連情報がどこまで積み上がっているかは、
各金融政策決定会合での判断材料の厚みにも影響する。 
 まず、2026 年10 月会合の段階では、2027 年春闘について得られる具体的な情報は限られる。 
連合の「春季生活闘争基本構想」によって労働側の基本スタンスは確認できるほか、短観や支店
長会議などを通じて、企業収益や賃上げを取り巻く環境を把握することはできる。ただし、経団
連「経労委報告」の原案や主要産別の具体的な要求方針はまだ判明しておらず、春闘に関する解
像度はまだ高くない。このため、10 月時点では高い賃上げが続く環境が維持されているかを確認
する段階にとどまると位置づけられる。 
これに対し、12 月になると春闘の解像度は大きく高まる。経労委報告原案から経営側の姿勢が
明らかになり、主要産別の要求方針案、政労使会議、民間シンクタンクの予測なども出そろって
くる。早期に翌年度の賃上げを表明する企業も現れ始めるため、労使双方の姿勢や具体的な要求
水準から、翌年も高い賃上げが続くかどうかをかなりの程度判断できるようになる。 
 この点では、2025 年の金融政策運営が参考になる。植田総裁は同年10 月会見で、春闘について
最終妥結の賃金上昇率が判明するまで待つのではなく、 「初動のモメンタム」を確認したいと説明
していた。その後、12 月会合では、労使の対応方針や日銀の本支店ヒアリングなどを踏まえ、翌
年も「しっかりとした賃上げが実施される可能性が高い」と判断し、利上げに踏み切った。春闘
の最終結果を待たなくても、年内に初動のモメンタムを確認できれば、それが政策判断を後押し
し得ることを示した例といえる。 
１月まで待てば、判断材料はさらに増える。 年末年始の早期賃上げ表明や新年祝賀会での経営
者発言に加え、１月の支店長会議では、中小企業を含めた賃上げ姿勢について企業ヒアリング情
報を得ることができる。12 月までに春闘の方向感はかなり見えてくる一方、１月にはその見方を
より幅広い企業情報によって確認することが可能になる。 
したがって、春闘という観点からみれば、10 月は「高い賃上げが続く環境が維持されている
か」を確認する段階、12 月は「初動のモメンタムをかなり具体的に把握できる段階」 、１月は「そ
の見方をより幅広い情報で確認する段階」と整理できる。2025 年の経験を踏まえれば、日銀は必

### Page 8

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
8/8 
ずしも３月の春闘結果まで待つ必要はない。賃金の観点からは、年内に十分強い初動のモメンタ
ムを確認できるかどうかが、12 月と１月の政策判断を分ける一つのポイントになり得る。 
このように、今後段階的に春闘動向や関連経済指標の情報が積み上がり、それに応じて賃金上
昇の持続性に対する判断も深まっていくことになる。足元では物価上振れリスクが金融政策判断
の中心にあるが、春闘動向も利上げのタイミングやペースに影響を与え得る。本稿で解説した春
闘関連のイベントについて、注意深く確認していくことが重要になるだろう。

## Document 158: Allianz_2026-09-08_Ten top of mind questions on the bond market rout_f5e219b4

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-09-08_Ten top of mind questions on the bond market rout_f5e219b4.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-09-08
Pages: 15
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary 
The bond market dominates the financial news flow since summer. Here, we answer the ten top-
of-mind questions about the latest market trend: 
1. Is the bond market in panic mode? No. Sovereign yields rose since July, again reaching multi-
year highs, but the speed of increase was lower (30-50bp) than after the start of the US-Iran war 
(50-90bp). Bond markets are still functioning well  according to bid-ask spreads or auction 
demand, but elevated rate levels are rightfully raising debt sustainability concerns. 
2. Have higher energy prices contributed to the recent sell -off? Yes. Renewed tensions in the 
Middle East since July and destructions of refining capacity in Russia have raised energy costs in 
particular gas prices again, leading to higher  inflation and central bank expectations  (Fed 
terminal rate +35bp, ECB +50bp) explaining the lion’s share of rate increases. Europe’s energy 
dependence is back in focus, with inflation rising to 3.3% in August, above the May reading.  
3. Can we blame AI funding for the lack of demand in government bonds? Partially. Even 
though total net credit issuance, largely due to AI investment, rose from USD48bn ytd last year  
to USD356bn ytd this year, equivalent to around half of US Treasuries' net supply of USD681bn 
ytd, stable swap spreads do not indicate strong crowding out lately. Structurally, credit spreads 
near decade-lows indicate that the supply imbalance has shifted against government bonds. 
4. Will Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's fiscal dominance attempts backfire? Yes if they 
intensify. Off-the-run bond b uybacks or using excess cash  to temporarily lower issuance  are 
accounting tricks that will  not lead to lower debt issuance in  the medium run. With an average 
debt maturity of just 5.9 years, the US remains highly exposed to refinancing conditions . 
Ultimately, only credible fiscal consolidation can ease pressure on yields.   
5. Could the US mid-term elections improve the fiscal policy outlook? No. But despite the likely 
political deadlock, we expect Democrats and Republicans to find a compromise on a modest 
fiscal tightening (0.5–0.6% of GDP/year), which would halve the pace of debt accumulation to 
+1pp of GDP/year through 2028, but would not stabilize the debt ratio . 
6. Can Fed Chair Kevin Warsh calm the markets if bond markets go south? Yes, he has already 
done so in Jackson Hole, by re -affirming the Fed’s commitment to bring down inflation . 
Moreover, the Fed has  unlimited firepower  to purchase bonds to lower yields. But this would 
only happen if market functioning were at risk given Warsh’s reluctance to use QE.  
7. The USD is down, Bitcoin and Gold are up – is the debasement trade back?  Yes, but to a 
lesser extent than in 2025 following Liberation Day . After a war-driven reversal in H1 2026, the 
“hard” basket (precious metals) has outperformed the “soft” basket (govies, equity and inflation 
breakevens) by +15pps since July as concerns over the USD resurfaced.  
8. Are European bonds suffering from rising political instability?  Yes. Governments are 
perceived as less likely to deliver necessary but painful fiscal tightening ahead of upcoming 
elections amid a rise of populistic parties across the continent. France and Italy are the clearest 
pressure point with the OAT-Bund spread at 86bp with 100bp possible by year end. 
9. What’s the role of Japan in all this?  It adds to the turmoil as we expect the BoJ to hike by 
+75bp more by end -2027 (to 1.75%), encouraging Japanese capital repatriation . Japanese 
investors hold around USD1.1tn of US Treasuries, while France is also particularly exposed to 
foreign portfolio flows, with non-residents holding around 56% of its government debt.   
10. Bond outlook: Could it get worse before it gets better? Yes. While current yield levels look 
attractive, the negative feedback loop between higher rates and debt sustainability could 
trigger self-fulfilling prophecies in several countries (FR, IT, UK, JP, US). We estimate temporary 
yield spikes of up to 70bp before central banks would weigh in. But overall, we consider risks as 
balanced as any demand side shock would rapidly lower yields.

Allianz Research | 1 September 2026 
Ten top-of-mind questions on the bond market rout

### Page 2

2

1. Is the bond market in panic mode?  
No, but multi-year highs have caught investor attention again . Interest r ates on government bonds have  
increased again in summer and reached multi-year highs. In most western economies 10y rates have reached levels 
last seen in 2007-2011 (Germany, France, UK) while in Japan they are the highest in 20 years. Notably, the very long 
end of government bond yield curves has also risen strongly. While US 10y rates are still below their 2023 high the 
30y rate has crossed 5.3% - the highest level since 2007  (see Figure 1).  However, while the long -run picture looks 
eye-watering, it is important to highlight that the increase in interest rates over the summer months was not 
particularly fast and therefore does not imply panic selling by investors. In fact, the increase in 10y rates since July 
ranged from +30bp in the US to +55bp in France. While substantial , this is lower than the increase following the 
start of the US-Iran war until mid-May when the range was within +55bp for Germany and +90bp for UK (see Figure 
2). Also, auction demand, bid-offer spreads and other liquidity indicators do not show any sign that the bond market 
is not functioning anymore. Rather because the increases since July have again surpassed recent multi-year highs, 
they have triggered media and investor attention. This is justified because interest rate levels are indeed questioning 
debt sustainability . But we are not (yet) in a “Truss moment,” scenario, or a market panic and rapid sell -off, 
prompting central bank intervention, such as that triggered by then British Prime Minister Liz Truss in September 
2022. 
The latest  bond sell -off cannot be pinned down to one trigger but rather a combination of forces. The most 
dominant driver was renewed geopolitical tensions and higher energy prices (Question 2) . But other factors also 
played a role:  a crowding -out effect of government bond demand due to high corporate issuance related to AI 
(Question 3), unstructured attempts by the US Treasury (Question 4) to ease issuance pressure due to high fiscal 
deficit (Question 5), and misleading communication by the Fed that was, however, at least partially restored during 
the Jackson Hole conference (Question 6). Investors also dislike rising political instability in Europe which magnifies 
stretched fiscal budgets (Question 8) and fiscal slippage, as well as the late hawkish turnaround by the BoJ in Japan 
(Question 9).

Figure 1: Government bond yields have risen to the highest levels in years or even decades (Japan).

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

2. Have higher energy prices contributed to the recent sell-off?  
Yes. Renewed tensions in the Middle East and the destructions of refining capacity in Russia have raised energy 
costs and inflation expectations. Figure 2 shows that rates co -moved with oil prices  in particular when looking at 
medium term expectations (12-month forward oil price). While the increase in the dated brent price during summer 
(peak close to 103USD/bbl) was significantly below the Q2 peak (144), markets started to price in a longer duration 
of the conflict or at least of oil supply disruptions  with the 12m forward oil price reaching similar ranges as in Q2  – 
although slightly lower as market participants are starting to factor-in new bypasses and substitutions (78 USD/bbl 
on average in Q2 vs 75 USD/bbl on average since July) . Gas prices increased even more strongly over the summer 
as did refined products due to Ukraine’s targeting of the refining industry in Russia. This weighed particularly on 
European inflation expectations and rates.

### Page 3

3

Figure 2: In 2026, government bond yields co-moved with longer-term oil price expectations.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

Higher inflation expectations shifted anticipated central bank policy rates up – a major driver for the increase in 
longer dated bond yields. Figure 3 shows how the rise in the expected policy rate s at the end of 2027, proxied by 
money market futures, co-moved closely with 10y government bond yields. With the breakdown of the US -Iran 
ceasefire and renewed worries about energy supply and inflation markets priced in an additional 50bp of hikes for 
the ECB and 35bp for the Fed since July – ultimately pushing also 10y rates higher by 35bp (DE) and 25bp (US).

Figure 3: Higher expected central bank policy rates pushed bond yields higher in the US (LHS) and Europe (RHS)

Sources: Bloomberg, Allianz Research 
Notes: Sofr Future Z7 and Euribor 3m Future Z7 are used as a proxy for the expected policy rate in Dec 2027.

Stable swap spreads over the summer suggest that fiscal concerns have not been the main driver of rising 
yields. Ten-year swap spreads measure the difference between the expected cumulative central bank policy rate 
over the next ten years and a country's government bond yield. An anticipated increase in bond supply, driven by 
rising fiscal deficits, typically pushes government bond yields above the expected policy rate, causing swap 
spreads to narrow. This phenomenon was clearly visible in Germany at the end of 2024, when the incoming 
government began discussing the abolition of the debt brake  or earlier that year in France, when Macron called 
snap elections. Over the summer of 2026, however, swap spreads in Germany, the US and the UK  (not France) 
remained broadly stable, indicating little change in expected bond issuance. This reinforces the view that rising 
inflation expectations, rather than fiscal concerns, were the primary driver of higher yields.

### Page 4

4

Figure 4: Swap spreads have been fairly stable in the second half of 2026 in the US, UK and Germany

Sources: Bloomberg, Allianz Research 
Notes: Swap spreads indicate expected supply-demand adjustments in the government bond market . The red 
circle highlights the time when discussions in Germany began to abolish the debt brake following the 202 5 
election. France continuously faces lower swap spreads meaning government bond yield rise more and more 
above swap yields.

3. Can we blame AI funding for the lack of demand in government bonds?  
Partially: AI borrowers have already raised USD356bn in US credit markets this year, compared with USD174bn 
in the whole of 2025, and because they have almost nothing maturing, essentially none of it is refinancing . Two 
thirds of investment grade issuance normally replace debt that is coming due, and the cash investors get back from 
maturing bonds pays for it. Here that cash does not exist. The hyperscalers have sold USD15 0bn worth of dollar 
bonds this year against USD2 3bn falling due before the end of 2027, so the money has to  come from somewhere 
else. AI credit issuance was worth a ninth of the Treasury's net coupon supply last year; against the USD681bn raised 
so far this year it is worth half. They are also borrowing long, with USD285bn falling due only in 2035 or later (Figure 
4), and their share of the index has risen from around 2.6% a year ago to 5.3% now. 
Figure 4: Hyperscalers have almost nothing to refinance, so the issuance is net new (Hyperscaler bond maturities 
by year, USD bn)

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Treasury swap spreads have not moved this year, the investment grade index is 2bps wider, and the large AI 
borrowers are 34bps wider. The swap spread is the cleanest of the three because it strips out the level of rates and 
isolates demand for government paper itself: at -44bps it sits where it started in January (Figure 5). Inside the AI 
cohort the widening lands where the paper is, with bonds priced this year around 40bps wider at the long end since 
pricing against 15bps at the short end, and Nvidia's 2056 issue 43bps wider two months after it was sold.

Figure 5: The AI cohort has paid the concession, and nobody else has (Change since January 2026, bps)

Notes: The AI cohort is a constant sample of large AI-related benchmark bonds; the Treasury swap spread is 10 -

AI borrowers have paid the concession themselves, and the spillover to everyone else is moderate.  Strip the AI 
cohort out of the index and what remains trades at 79bps, against a median of 128bps since 1997, with only 24 of 
the past 356 months tighter. Neither big sector has moved either: financials are 2bps wider this year and industrials 
1bp, against 34bps for the AI names. Banks are still the largest individual borrowers in the index, with seven of them 
in the top ten holding USD955bn between them against USD543bn for the whole AI cohort. With almost nothing to 
refinance, each year of AI issuance adds to the stock rather than replacing it . What to watch is the concentration: 
the AI cohort has doubled its share of the index in a year, from 2.6% to 5.3%, and nothing in the maturity profile stops 
it going further.

4. Will Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's fiscal dominance attempts backfire?   
Yes, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent’s attempts to limit the rise in bond yields could backfire if he doubles down 
on technical measures such as expanded buybacks or TGA -funded purchases.  The Treasury announced an 
increase in long -dated bonds buybacks on 19 August, subsequently topping up the size of the scheme. Recently, 
several senior Treasury officials have hinted that the Treasury General Account (TGA) – the government’s cash 
deposit held at the Fed – could be used to fund these purchases (in place of, or a s a substitute for, bills issuance). 
These announcements have not had a sustained impact on long -dated bond yields, signaling skepticism amongst 
market participants. Doubling down on sticker platers solutions, and continuing to claim that higher yields reflect 
technical fac tors rather than fundamentals (i.e. higher fiscal and monetary policy uncertainty) could give the 
perception that the executive power is not likely to reduce the fiscal deficit – in our view the only sensible policy to 
lower pressure on long-term yields. The US debt is exposed to changing financing conditions and rollover risks, given 
that the weighted average maturity is only 5.9 years. It also means that funding costs are closely tied to Fed policy, 
which increases fiscal dominance risks. With the political deadlock likely to increase after the November midterms 
elections (see below), a sizeable fiscal consolidation is unlikely. However, we think a modest fiscal consolidation 
(0.5%-0.6% of GDP) will be agreed between the Republicans and the Democrats which will slow down the increase 
of the debt ratio. The savings could entrail a mix of discretionary and social spending restraint and ‘stealth’ rise of 
taxation (see below). Ultimately, if Bessent remains concerned abo ut the long end, he will likely reduce issuance in 
the next quarterly refunding process, but more sticky platers solutions cannot be ruled out. A concerning 
development would be the Treasury starting to run down its TGA ahead of a potential debt ceiling st andoff next 
year. In the latest debt ceiling spat in 2023, the Treasury largely ran down the TGA (to 23bn by June 2023) to continue 
financing government spending. Hypothetically, if we get into 2027 with few TGA buffers left, it is not clear how the 
Democrats would handle the debt ceiling: moderate their demands amid risks that the federal government is 
running short on buffers, or ratchet up pressure on the Republicans to extract concessions? The TGA level currently 
sits at around 5.5 weeks of total withdrawals, excluding public debt redemptions and revenues.

5. Could the US mid-term elections improve the fiscal policy outlook? 
No. Rising interest expenses and mildly expansionary fiscal policy are expected to increase the deficit modestly 
this year, albeit at a high level . The White House spending package is likely to be watered down.  The federal 
government deficit decreased significantly in 2025 under the first year of the second Trump administration . On a 
cash-based measure, it settled at -5.4% of GDP versus -6.9% in 2024  (versus -7.2% GDP in 2024 and -6.4% GDP in 
2025 for the national -accounts measure)  1. Sharply higher tariffs and spending restraint were the two main 
contributors of deficit reduction. However, this year we expect the (cash -based measure) deficit to widen again, 
though mildly so, at -5.7% of GDP. First, the OBBB Act delivered tax cuts for h ouseholds and corporates. Second, 
IEEAP tariffs were struck down by the Supreme Court in February, leading the federal government to pay out 
refunds to importers and corporates. Meanwhile, spending restraint has been moderate so far this year and interest 
expenses continue to grind higher. Against a backdrop of increased scrutiny over public finances, the latest 
USD100bn White House spending package , comprising mostly new defense and border enforcement spending , 
looks less likely to pass the Senate in full this autumn. Republican fiscal hawks are likely to stall the bill unless, unless 
the White House agrees to offsetting savings measures.

Political deadlock and a likely divided Congress after the November midterm elections mean that US fiscal 
policy is unlikely to turn significantly restrictive, with Bessent’s 3% of GDP deficit target in 2028 out of reach.  
Treasury Secretary Bessent has acknowledged that dynamic Social Security and Medicare spending are structural 
problems that need to be addressed to put the US debt trajectory on a sustainable path. On the other hand , the 
Democrats see tax hikes a s the main avenue to reduce the deficit. Entitlement reforms to Social Security and 
Medicare need 60 Senate votes to pass, which the Republicans do not currently have, and are even less likely to 
reach after the midterm elections in November. We place a 75% probability that the Democrats win the House (out 
of which 40% that they get the Senate , too), versus 25% that the Republicans keep both chambers (Table 1).  
Likewise, any attempt by the Democrats to pass tax hikes will likely face Republican pushback in Congress. In this 
environment, we would expect only limited fiscal consolidation in 2027 and 2028, to the tune of 0.5%/0.6% of GDP 
per year of structural deficit improvement, relying on a mix of discretionary and social spending restraint and 
‘stealth’ taxation increase.

1 The cash-based measure is sourced from the Bureau of the Fiscal Service. The alternative, national-accounts measure (sourced from the BEA) 
reports a deficit which shrunk from -7.2% GDP in 2024 to -6.4 GDP in 2025. The latter deficit is an accrual and a nat ional-accounts measure: it 
times flows when the liability is incurred (taxes accrued, interest accrued rather than paid) and excludes purely financial transactions.

Any fiscal consolidation in a divided Congress would likely rely on modest, politically palatable spending 
restraint, rather than major entitlement reform. On discretionary spending, Democrats and Republicans could 
agree on tighter caps, slower federal hiring and pay growth, and efficiency savings, building on the precedent of 
the 2011 Budget Control Act. On mandatory spending, bipartisan support may be more feasible for targeted 
measures – such as Medicare provider payments, Medicare Advantage overpayments, or higher premiums for 
affluent beneficiaries – than for broad benefit cuts. More limited savings could come from targeted reductions in 
agricultural subsidies and other politically protected programs. On the revenue side, bracket creep could provide a 
further, less politically visible boost to receipts as nominal incomes rise faster than tax thresholds.  Overall, such 
measures could deliver gradual consolidation while avoiding contentious cuts to Social Security, Medicaid and core 
Medicare benefits.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, CBO, Allianz Research 
Note: federal public debt held by the public. Stock flows adjustments, which are difficult to predict, could alter the 
forecasts significantly.

Limited fiscal consolidation under a divided Congress , as we expect , should slow down the increase in public 
debt over the next two years. But spikes in short -term interest rates could pressure the White House  and 
Congress to embark on more aggressive fiscal tightening. Under the assumptions of limited fiscal consolidation, 
the federal primary deficit could reach -1.3% of GDP by 2028 (from -2.3% expected this year). If short -term interest 
rates do not increase much – most of the US debt is issued on short-end maturities, making it more sensitive to short-
term rates – the-debt-to-GDP ratio increase could be limited to around +1pp per year through 2028 – slower than 
in recent years (+2pp per year on average in 2023-25, Figure 6). Meanwhile, interest expenses would stabilize, albeit 
at an elevated level, both as a share of GDP and of  federal revenues (Figure 7). But if real interest rates were to 
spike (e.g. the Fed hiking rates aggressively to tame inflation), interest expenses would rise again, eating further 
into government revenues, increasing the risk of a fiscal crisis and event ually pressuring the administration and 
Congress to embark on more aggressive fiscal tightening. Under this scenario, nominal GDP growth could likely 
slow, providing no offset to the debt -to-GDP-ratio increase. Alternatively, the higher nominal GDP growth that we 
expect – for instance induced by stronger AI effects – could improve debt sustainability and ease pressure on the 
government to reduce the structural deficit. Real GDP growth would need to be sustainably above +3% to stabi lize 
the debt -ratio under our mild fiscal consolidation scenario (against +2% expected in our baseline, taking into 
account a gradual AI-induced pick up in productivity growth , partially offsetting worsening demographics). Higher 
inflation could also help, but the favorable effect on debt dynamics would likely be partially offset by higher interest 
rates.

6. Can Fed Chair Kevin Warsh calm the markets if bond markets go south?  
Yes. He proved so in Jackson Hole, where he re-affirmed the Fed’s commitment to bring down inflation. The Fed 
has unlimited firepower, in principle, to purchase bonds, but this would likely only happen if market functioning 
were at risk . However, clear communication from the Fed Chair on short -term interest policy would help limit 
the rise of long -dated government bond premium s. A sharp sell -off in government bonds – 50-100bps yields 
increase in the space of a few days – could prompt the Fed to launch unlimited purchases, citing unwarranted rate 
moves and risks to financial stability. This could trigger a  sudden loss of trust in debt sustainability , prompting a 
rapid sell-off in rates, similar to the “Truss moment” in the UK in 2022 (see Question 10). As long as the rise in bond 
yields is more orderly and reflects rising term premium over inflation and/or fiscal risks, then the Fed’s best policy 
would be to let financial conditions tighten, and  follow through with rate hikes to ease concerns over inflation risks 
and monetary policy uncertainty. In the near term, a sensible course of action for Warsh is to reduce uncertainty 
around the Fed’s reaction function. A near -term risk would be for Warsh to announce a premature easing of policy 
– for instance on the premise that higher-long term yields could justify a lower policy rate. Such a move, which could 
erode the Fed’s anti -inflation credibility and push up long -term bond yields, seems unlikely after his speech in 
Jackson Hole. In our June baseline scenario, we expect a rate hike from the Fed in September (+25bp to 4%), but it 
could come later. This would mean that the prime rate charged by banks to households and companies would rise 
by more than 20bps just one month later, as the pass-through is rather quick in the US.

Figure 8: Pass-through from key interest rates to short-term bank rates

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

7. The USD is down, Bitcoin and gold are up – is the debasement trade back? 
In August a familiar pattern from 2025 has re-emerged: Rising yields, a weaker dollar and stronger real assets 
such as gold and Bitcoin  – next to equities .  After a war -driven reversal in H1 2026, those “debasement assets ” 
have seen significant interest again with a particular boost after Bessent announced unconvincing measures to curb 
debt issuance (see question 4).  This is a strong reminder of 2025, following Liberation Day and subsequent threats 
by Bessent to charge a “revenge tax” on foreign investors – another policy that global investors disliked.

Figure 9: Bitcoin and gold have risen again, the USD has weakened over the summer

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

### Page 10

10

Since Covid, and with repeated inflation shocks – the post -pandemic rebound, Ukraine, tariffs, Iran – the 
"debasement" theme has regained centrality in macro analysis.  Debasement is defined as the declining value of 
money, captured simultaneously across three prices: the exchange rate, interest rates, and inflation. When the 
policy mix is perceived as expansionary, or when exogenous shocks hit, these three monetary prices tend to devalue 
in tandem. To quantify this, we construct two baskets:  the first one is a "Supply constrained Basket" ("hard assets"): 
long gold / silver / platinum . The second one is a  "Monetary Policy Sensitive Basket" ("soft assets"): long US 
breakevens / 30y-5y steepener / long S&P 500 / short FX. 2

Figure 10: Hard vs Soft Debasement basket performance (Base Jan 2025 = 100)

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Precious metals are the most reliable barometer of debasement than rates, breakevens, or risk assets. A 
conventional policy-driven debasement – expansionary settings, rising inflation expectations, a steepening curve, 
dollar weakness – would show up primarily in the soft basket; instead, it has consistently underperformed. This 
suggests debasement is better read as a structural erosion of trust in fiat currency, expressed through tangible 
stores of value, than as a conventional repricin g of the policy mix. Beyond narratives around de -dollarization and 
central bank gold accumulation, markets appear to be pricing elevated sovereign debt and doubts over 
governments' willingness to control it. This is a signal that hard assets have been capturing constantly. The “hard” 
basket (gold, silver, platinum) strongly outperformed in 2025 (+101pps), before the war -driven inflation repricing 
temporarily reversed the trend in H1 2026 ( -15pps); since July, however, the 2025 regime has reasserted itself, with 
the hard basket outperforming again by +15pps in just two months as concerns over the USD return. This is precisely 
what the summer price action has been telling us. Gold and silver rebounded, while the dollar stayed on the back 
foot. The classic inflation-trade signature was absent. Instead, the pressure showed up at the long end, with curves 
steepening on term premium. Hard assets bid, the front end anchored, the long end penalized: that is a credibility 
repricing, not a policy-mix repricing, consistent with the long-term trend toward debasement.  
Figure 11 : Changes in foreign holdings of US treasuries since COVID (USD bn)

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

2 Indices are constructed from equally-weighted daily returns, geometrically compounded from a base of 100 at 2 January 2025. The Hard 
basket is long gold, silver and platinum (one third each). The Soft basket is long US 10-year breakevens, long the S&P 500, short USD (via 
inverted DXY) and long the 30y–5y curve steepener (one quarter each).

### Page 11

11

To explain this debasement index, we can find a strong relationship with the foreigner holdings of US treasuries. 
The regional breakdown clarifies a picture that look s more diffuse at the country level.  Asia/Pacific is the only 
region with a large net negative since Covid, and within it, selling is concentrated in China and Japan , while most 
other Asian holders (Taiwan, Singapore, India, South Korea) are net buyers or flat. Latin America & the Caribbean 
is marginally negative, driven by Brazil, though this is partly offset by acquisitions from the Cayman/Bermuda 
havens booked in the same region. Europe (Luxembourg, France, Belgium, UK) and the offshore financial centers 
(Cayman, Virgin Islands) are the dominant net buyers, by a wide margin. The more accurate framing isn't "surplus 
countries are exiting Treasuries ," it's that a small number of large official holders (China, Japan, and to a lesser 
extent Brazil) are net sellers. At the same time, a majority of foreign holders are simply not expanding purchases at 
the pace of new issuance,  while a growing share of net demand is being absorbed domestically or via 
offshore/allied intermediaries. This is where the debasement index comes into play;  the highest -impact sellers 
(China, Japan, foreign official generally) are having their yield effect muted by issuance -mix management and 
buybacks, which  is exactly the mechanism that suppress es the soft basket's response to declining foreign UST 
demand. Gold and other hard assets have no equivalent "buyer of last resort" to smooth their price discovery . 
Instead, the debasement signal that should be distributed across both baskets is being structurally routed into the 
hard-asset basket by default .. We do not know if Treasuries saw a significant selloff this summer, a s the data is 
delayed by two  months. We do see, however, that foreign investors have largely bought equities (real assets), 
instead of Treasuries, confirming the picture of the longer -term debasement trend.

Figure 12: Net purchases of US assets by foreign investors per asset class, bln USD

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research – last data point June 2026

8. Are European bonds suffering from rising political instability?  
Yes. European governments are perceived as less likely to deliver sustained fiscal tightening which would put 
debt levels on a firm downward trajectory. Our political fragility indices 3 (Figure 1 3) remain at elevated levels.  
Especially in Germany, it hit its highest level since the index began in 2008. The governing coalition in Berlin has 
been steadily losing ground since it formed last year. The far-right Alternative for Germany, or AfD, is now clearly 
the largest single party, well ahead of Chancellor Friedrich Merz’s  governing Christian Democratic Union. In Italy , 
the index is at its highest level since 2022, driven by the rise of the new far -right party Futuro Nazionale. FN has 
overtaken the right-wing Lega party, which is a junior partner in Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni’s governing coalition. 
That split has eroded the governing coalition as a whole . The index has been quite stable in France and Spain, 
though France remains by some distance the most fragile. The government is likely to struggle once again to pass 
a budget before the end of the year amid a fractured National Assembly. There are already risks of fiscal slippages 
this year, with the deficit potentially coming out at -5.2% GDP against -5.0% targeted by the government. For 2027, 
government officials have indicated that the deficit target could be set at -4.9% GDP, implying little fiscal 
improvement. Important to note is that without any additional fiscal consolidation measures, the fiscal deficit could 
reach -5.6% of GDP in 2027 because of dynamic underlying social spending. Meanwhile the Presidential elections 
of April 2027 are rapidly approaching. Anti -establishment parties Rassemblement National and La France 
Insoumise are high in the polls and it cannot be rule out that they will be both qualified to the 2 nd round. These 
parties are perceived as less credible to restore sound public finances. If these trends persist, term premium on

3 See 2026_07_23_PoliticalFragility.pdf

12

French bonds could further increase in 2027, exacerbated by a large share of foreign holders (56%). In the UK, 
political fragility has receded since the arrival of A. Burnham in July. The Labour Party has recovered in polls is now 
almost at the same level with Reform UK. However, bond markets will have to digest the upcoming Autumn Budget. 
Burnham has clearly stated his intention to increase government spending. How much of them will be funded with 
tax increases remain to be seen. Markets’ tolerance to new borrowing is very limited, and we would expect Burnham 
to tread carefully by switching from a moderate fiscal tightening (0.6% GDP in 2027, 0.4% GDP in 2028) planned 
under his predecessor to a ‘’soft fiscal’’ tightening (0.2% GDP and 0.3%GDP in both years).

9. What’s the role of Japan in all this?  
Japan is experiencing an imported inflationary episode, driven by higher energy and commodity costs alongside 
a structurally weak yen.  The resulting pressure on the Bank of Japan to tighten policy is not just about inflation 
control; it is about maintaining credibility in both the FX and government bond markets. As Japanese yields rise, the 
economics of overseas investment become less co mpelling. Capital that spent years financing global markets can 
increasingly be repatriated. This matters because Japan sits at the heart of the global dollar recycling system, acting 
simultaneously as a major lender of dollar liquidity and one of the larg est foreign buyers of US Treasuries. The 
consequence is potentially profound: a tighter BOJ does not only tighten financial conditions in Japan; it withdraws 
liquidity from the rest of the world. Should Japanese investors accelerate their repatriation flows, Treasury demand 
would weaken precisely when US financing needs remain elevated. In an extreme scenario, outright sales of 
Treasuries could emerge as institutions raise liquidity to support domestic markets. A weaker yen is forcing a hike to 
gain back control on FX, which is tightening global financial conditions and push up global rates higher. The result 
was a powerful transmission channel from Tokyo to Washington. What starts as upward pressure on Japanese rates

### Page 13

can rapidly become upward pressure on US yields. Hence, rising Japanese rates could become a thorn in the foot of 
US yields. Stabilizing the Treasury market will therefore be closely tied to stabilizing the yen and preventing 
domestic Japanese adjustment to have a worldwide impact.  
We expect the BOJ to deliver one additional hike to 1.25% by the end of the year before reaching a terminal rate 
of 1.75% by the end of 2027. Despite short term tailwind from the recent interventions and the BOJ tightening cycle, 
we continue to expect the yen to remain around 160 USD/JPY in the near term. Meanwhile, aggressive quantitative 
tightening (QT) continues, pushing long -term yields significantly higher. Table 2 shows that, while Japan has the 
highest debt-to-GDP ratio among developed economies, its central bank also holds the largest share of government 
bonds among the countries in the sample. The current pace of balance -sheet reduction is resulting in a substantial 
supply of JGBs that must be absorbed by private investors.

10. Bond outlook: Could it get worse before it gets better?  
Yes, self-fulfilling prophecy reactions in which higher yields trigger higher risk premia on debt sustainability 
concerns cannot be ruled out in multiple countries. With current yield levels at decade highs in many countries , 
government bonds look attractive to investors. However, given the negative feedback loop from higher rates to 
debt sustainability, investors could become more nervous in the near term and demand a higher risk premium which 
leads to higher rates and therefore reinforces the downward spiral (self-fulfilling prophecy reaction). A sudden loss 
of trust in debt sustainability could lead to a rapid sell -off in rates within days or even hours, like the Truss moment 
in the UK in 2022 (see Figure 15). The Bank of England responded by stepping in to limit the rise in Gilt yields, which 
increased by +130bp in just three trading days , following the September 2022 Budget announcement . Future 
triggers could include misleading communication from central banks or treasur y departments , extreme election 
outcomes or polls, a rating decision4, or, on a more technical level, weaker demand at a government bond auction.  
Figure 15: The BoE intervened in 2022’s Truss moment when 10y UK yields climbed >100bp in five days

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Fiscal health metrics highlight stresses in various countries for different reasons.  Table 2 highlights which fiscal 
metrics require close monitoring when assessing fiscal vulnerabilities.  Several countries stand out. Japan has the 
highest share of government debt, although much of it is held by the Bank of Japan (BoJ). With a primary deficit of 
2.9% of GDP, France stands out negatively in terms of fiscal discipline. The US appears particula rly concerning in 
terms of the share of government receipts devoted to debt servicing. However, this is also a function of 
comparatively low tax rates. Were it to raise taxes, it could easily reach debt -servicing levels, as a share of GDP, 
closer to those of Italy or th e UK. Germany, France and Spain have a large proportion of their debt held abroad, 
which is typically viewed positively. Foreign holders of government debt are often sovereign wealth funds, central 
banks or other large institutional investors that tend not to ad just their allocations rapidly.  A key metric for long -
term debt sustainability is the well-known r − g < 0 relationship, which captures the condition that real interest rates 
should be lower than real economic growth to prevent a country from entering a debt spiral. On this measure, 
France and the UK appear the most vulnerable. By contrast, the US, supported by strong real growth and relatively 
low real interest rates, remains in a fairly safe position.

4 Several rating agencies will publish their sovereign rating reviews in September-November 2026.

But “central bank puts” remain in place  covering worst case scenarios even in times of elevated inflation.  
However, even in times of elevated inflation, central banks would not hesitate to intervene . We expect a threshold 
of around 70bp within a couple of days for them to act. The ECB has created the Transmission Protection Instrument 
(TPI), which would allow substantial quantitative easing (QE) if "market functioning" were to be threatened. A milder 
first step would be to halt the current record pace of quantitative tightenin g (QT) at around EUR40bn per month. 
Similarly, the Fed or the BoJ could restart QE  (see Question 6) , just as the BoE did in 2022 following the 
announcement of an unsustainable fiscal 'mini' budget, or as the Fed did in 2023 following the SVB banking crisis. 5 
Such a move could come even in times of elevated inflation because QE would support market functioning while 
policy rates could remain unchanged to keep the short end of the yield curve elevated.   
Overall, however, the outlook for rates is balanced. Any demand side shock to the US or global economy 
would trigger lower rates immediately – painful for those who exited the bond market at elevated yield levels. 
An increase in taxes, government spending cuts or a financial market correction could all be trigger-points. A more 
prominent example of a demand side shock could be the bursting of the AI boom because a subsequent 
slowdown of fixed investment and household consumption (which has been driven by wealth effects from higher 
equity prices) would lead to a sharp economic slowdown and disinflation. In such a scenario – central banks 
would lower policy rates pulling government bond yields down  as well.

## Document 161: Allianz_2026-09-17_How the AI supercycle is reshaping global interest rates_f60e61d1

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-09-17_How the AI supercycle is reshaping global interest rates_f60e61d1.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary 
• The AI supercycle will pull long -term yields down in both the US and Europe , but for 
very different reasons and by very different paths. We model this across five transmission 
channels – three macro (neutral rate, inflation expectations, fiscal collateral) and two 
micro (AI -driven duration supply and crowding -out) – applied to an AI upside and 
downside scenario. Over a 10-year horizon the net effect is around −50bp for both regions 
in the upside scenario (roughly +5pp of cumulated US potential growth, +1.6pp for the 
eurozone), and a prolonged, smaller drag in the downside (−2.5pp and −1.3pp 
respectively). The US result depends on AI producti vity actually materializing to keep its 
fiscal trajectory intact; the eurozone gets to the same place even without the AI dividend, 
since its fiscal position doesn't need repairing in the first place. The US is the levered, 
volatile expression of the AI trade; Europe is the safer one. 
• The market has already priced the AI productivity story for the US, but not the fiscal 
relief that should come with it. The USD 5y1y forward has moved from ~3.0% to 4.9% since 
mid-2025 some 150bp above our median neutral -rate estimate and 90bp above the top  
of our estimate range, and more than the full +67bp our model assigns to the Ai enhanced 
neutral-rate range over the next decade. What isn't priced at all is the fiscal collateral 
improvement, worth −85bp to the US 10Y by the end of the horizon. Either th e market 
doesn't believe AI will repair the fiscal trajectory, or it's pricing a less favorable version of 
the same story with higher AI -driven job displacement pushing up social protection and 
unemployment spending enough to offset the tax-revenue gain. 
• Fiscal collateral – the extent to which a stronger economy makes a government's debt 
easier to repay – is what actually decides the outcome, in both scenarios and both 
regions. The transatlantic rates converging seems counterintuitive at first view as Europe's 
growth impulse is a third the size of the US. But its fiscal position benefits more, because 
the improvement lands on a stable debt path rather than the expansionary US pat h. For 
every point of growth AI delivers, Europe's fiscal position improves abou t 1.6 times more 
than that of the US. 
• The way AI interacts with interest rate isn't a straight path, and its shape differs on each 
side of the Atlantic.  Both curves rise well above their eventual resting point before 
reversing, the US 10Y's round trip is 135bp (versus 80bp for the eurozone) even though the 
net 10 -year effect is only −50bp for both. The peak trade also looks different in each 
market: a butterfly in the US (buy the belly, sell the wings, as front-loaded micro effects hit 
the wings while macro effects lag into the belly) versus a flattener in Europe. By the end of 
the period both curves converge to a similar, milder flattening.

How the AI supercycle is reshaping global interest 
rates

### Page 2

2

AI is no longer only a sector story, but a macro-scale claim on the economy. 
Artificial intelligence (AI) has become a major macroeconomic force. AI investment will pass USD1tn globally in 
2026, of which more than USD500bn in the US alone . That is 1.8% of US GDP and 0.9% of world GDP, on a path to 
2.8% and 1.4% respectively by 2028.  In terms of growth, tech and data center activity will represent 36%-50% of US 
year-on-year growth this year. Globally, the direct AI investment impulse is worth roughly 10% of global growth. 
From the financial markets side,  AI has mostly been examined through the lens of  equities (sector analysis , 
valuation, concentration risk), or private credit  (risk profile and risk pricing of AI investments types (see our latest 
reports here linked here). This paper asks how the AI supercycle transmits into interest rates .. 
US and eurozone vary in the level of macro impact, but share similar profile 
We model the effect against a baseline without AI effects, measuring the difference versus an AI  upside scenario 
and one in which AI shifts to the downside. We use several published estimates to derive scenarios for the impact 
on potential growth, trend productivity and trend employment. We define an  upside scenario  with cumulated 
potential growth gains of +4.94pp in the US and +1.60pp in the eurozone; in the downside we assume -2.47pp in the 
US and -1.35pp in the eurozone. The US macro scenario shows much greater sensitivity to AI, while Europe has less 
to gain in terms of productivity . The pattern is similar for inflation, where in the upside scenario the US face s a 
stronger supply- and energy-driven uptick, before higher productivity gains take over and reverse the AI impact into 
a disinflationary trend. Inversely in the negative scenario, the negative AI demand shock brings US inflation further 
down than it does in the eurozone (Table 1).

Our framework rests on five major transmission channels 
We identify two major channels of transmission: 1) a macroeconomic channel through potential growth, inflation 
dynamics and the fiscal position (plus a spillover effect for the eurozone), and 2) a microeconomic channel through 
the changes AI financing brings to the structure of fixed income markets. The latter has gained importance recently 
as market participants have discussed the possibility that AI debt issuance was competing with US government debt 
and therefore contributing to driving up yields. The macro channels capture the changes in the way long-term rates 
discount economic variable s. The micro channels c apture how the AI funding model (market size, self-funded vs 
debt issuance) affects the price of duration. Macro and micro channels work on different lags, and their effects can 
change sign over our 10-year forecast horizon. Generally, the macro channels affect the expected rate component 
of long-term rates, while micro channels act on the term premium. The exception is the fiscal position which, despite 
being a macro channel, affects the term premium component of long term government bond yields.  
1) Neutral rate r*  is the real policy rate at which the economy neither overheats nor stalls. It is the level the 
expected path of short rates is anchored to. If AI raises trend productivity, capital earns more, firms want to 
invest more, and the neutral rate balancing saving against investment rises. A lasting productivity gain 
therefore lifts r*  which feeds through the entire yield curve. It has  a stronger effect at the front end as it 
transmits through expected short rates, and productivity gains have a long-term mean reverting trend. The 
effect is inverted in case AI would fail to produce productivity gains in an economy facing a debt overhang 
from AI investments.  
2) For inflation expectations we use an adaptive expectation approach where the estimation windows adjust 
with previous estimation errors. As AI has a strong supply impact at first, we get upside push in expectations

### Page 3

3

that fades with time when productivity gains take over . As the phase in becomes smoother, the change in 
expectation is less pronounced to the downside. These factors also influence the expected rate component 
of government bond yields. 
3) Fiscal collateral is defining the fundamental value  of sovereign bonds as discounted claims on future 
primary surpluses. The channel captures its change in response to an AI-driven up- or downside productivity 
shock. A productivity shock has an asymmetric effect: tax revenues improving the fiscal position are elastic 
to GDP, while the largest spending lines (pensions, benefits, debt service) are sticky.  
4) Duration supply  is a market -microstructure model tracking AI capex as a generator of new duration, 
through both long -dated IG bonds and pay -fixed swap structures in private credit. The curve effect sits at 
the long end  as 65% the supply lands beyond 10 years. The swap  spread effect  depends on fixed-rate 
issuance, which  cheapens the sovereign against swaps  while p ay-fixed hedging from priv ate credit, 
enrichens it. Duration supply affects the term premium, it is more pronounced at the longer end of the curve. 
5) Crowding Out is a sector -level displacement model affecting long -term rates via the swap-market. 
Financial institutions issue fixed-rate bonds and then ask for floating, receiving fixed in swaps. That receive-
fixed leg is how financial issuance moves duration out of the sovereign market. AI borrowers sit on the 
opposite side of the swap curve, paying fixed rather than receiving it . So both channels lift swap yields 
against the sovereign: one adds fixed -payer supply, the other removes fixed -receiver demand . Like 
duration supply, crowding-out affects the term premium at the longer end of the curve. 
6) Spillover Effect is added for the eurozone, acting mainly at the long end, where global term premia and 
rate levels remain structurally correlated and anchored to the US benchmark. It captures how AI effects 
propagate to global fixed income markets via the US Treasury market, even where AI exposur e is limited.

The AI super cycle is a bull market for duration, but it is not a directional trade 
Our results show that AI is a bull market for duration in both scenarios, but the path matters. In the upside scenario, 
AI lowers long-term rates by around 50bp over a 10-year horizon in both the US and the eurozone. In the downside 
scenario, rates are also held down for a prolonged period. In both cases, however, yields rise before they fall: the 
US 10Y makes a 135bp round trip in the upside and 72bp in the downside, against 80bp and 41bp for the eurozone. 
The AI effect on rates is therefore not a long-term directional trade, the right timing is essential. The timing depends 
on which channels dominate when, how long they take to act and where on the curve they bite hardest. That makes 
the shape of the curve as important as the yield level for positioning (Figure 1). 
Figure 1: Net AI effect on the 10Y yield – Upside and Downside scenarios 
in basis points

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

The end points for the US and the eurozone are remarkably close, even though the two start from very different 
positions in the AI race. The fiscal collateral channel explains why. Europe gets only a moderate growth impulse, 
but it lands on a stable fiscal position, which improves the value of its fiscal collateral significantly. The US gets a 
much larger growth impulse, yet the gain is partly offset by continued fiscal expansion over the next 10 years (we 
use the CBO forecast of debt/GDP rising 20pp over the next 10 years ). With a stable debt path, the increase in US 
fiscal collateral  value would subtract 125bp rather than 85 bp, lifting the total effect to -90bp and leaving the 
eurozone far behind. The transatlantic AI rates convergence is thus a late-cycle outcome: at the peak, the AI effect 
is more than twice as large in the US as in the eurozone.

The US curve rotates between the peak and the end of period due to micro effects 
At the peak of the upside scenario, the US curve shifts up, but with the middle of the curve (belly) holding up better: 
the 2s30s slope barely moves while 2s5s flattens 13bp and 5s30s steepens 12bp. The peak AI trade on the US curve 
is therefore a butterfly (buy the belly, sell the wings). In the eurozone the peak AI effect on the curve is a flattener 
instead: 2s30s −20bp, with the 2Y up 51bp against the 30Y up 31bp. This shape difference is driven by the stronger 
and front-loaded US micro channels, set against macro channels that arrive with a lag. In addition to the time lag, 
macro channels (neutral rate, inflation expectations) act mainly at the short end, while the micro channels hit the 
long end because AI issuance is long -dated. The 5Y sits in between: it absorbs little of the duration -supply and 
crowding-out pressure, yet is long enough to benefit from the improving fiscal collateral effect on its term premium. 
By the end of the period, both curves flatten to a similar degree  as fiscal collateral value becomes the dominant 
force, pulling the belly and the long end down while the 2Y is barely affected  (US 2s30s −41bp, EA −3 0bp) In the

Microeconomic channels and inflation move first, the fiscal channel decides the end point 
Having looked at the  way AI effect the  changes in yield levels and the influences the shape of the curve s, we now 
turn to the decomposition of the moves : which channels drives the yield and curve dynamics, how much each 
contributes and when. The sequence is the same in both scenarios: micro channels  and inflation expectations drive 
rates early on, while the fiscal channel and the neutral rate r* only kick in later.  
In the upside scenario, the peak arrives when productivity gains lift r* while inflation expectations are still elevated 
and the micro channels are still at work but before higher growth has significantly fed through to tax revenues rising 
the value of the fiscal collateral. For the US at the peak in 2032, r* alone contributes +67bp against −18bp from the 
fiscal channel. By the end point the picture reverses: the fiscal channel contributes −85bp, r* slows down to +42bp 
and inflation expectations flip to −18bp as the disinflationary effect of productivity gains sets in. Europe follows the 
same sequence at roughly half the amplitude, reflecting its lower sensitivity to AI.  
In the downside scenario, rates first move up, driven by inflation expectations on the back of AI investment and 
energy demand. In the US, the micro channels add another 20 -30bp from AI-related issuance. The tipping point is 
in 2031: productivity gains fail to materialize and the debt overhang starts weighing on growth, which later erodes 
tax revenues, increases government spending and reduces value of the fiscal collateral. In the eurozone the micro 
channels are negligible, but the spillover from US rates matters. 
The greater US sensitivity also shows up in the gross channel activity , i.e. the sum of all channel contributions in 
absolute terms. In the upside it amounts to 158bp in the US against 78bp in Europe for a broadly similar final 
outcome. In other words, much more happens under the surface in the US, but most of it cancels out: only 30% of the 
US channel activity is left once the channels are netted against each other, compared with 58% in Europe. The AI 
journey is therefore likely to be bumpier for US rates than for eurozone rates, even if both end up in a similar place. 
The AI effect should therefore translate into relatively higher US rates volatility  (Figure 4 & 5). 
Figure 4: Channel contributions to the 10Y  - Upside and Downside scenario 
in basis points

### Page 7

7

Figure 5: Channel contribution at every point of the curve: peak and terminal (basis points) 
In basis points

The fiscal channel has the last word – and the US needs the AI dividend more than Europe 
We find no transatlantic divide in the long-term AI effect on rates. At the end of the period the US and eurozone 10Y 
effects are only a few basis points apart in both scenarios. But the path to that point differs materially in level, drivers 
and timing. The convergence of the end points is attributable almost entirely to fiscal channel. Fiscal collateral 
contributes −85bp to the US terminal effect in the upside and −46bp to the eurozone, in both cases more than the 
channels working in the opposite direction, principally the neutral rate r* effect. 
The mechanism is the productivity beta of government debt. A positive productivity shock raises tax revenues by 
more than the GDP gain, so the present value of future primary surpluses improves and the bondholder is effectively 
long AI productivity. Europe is disadvantaged here, as it tax revenues are less sensitive to GDP (also because AI 
growth is tilted versus US and less taxable by European governments) but its spending is also more sticky, as Europe 
is more exposed to negative labor market effects caused by AI. On the other hand, the fiscal trajectory of Europe is 
more favorable, with a rather stable debt to GDP ratio projection of 86% for the next 10 years. Over the same time 
period, the CBO sees US debt growing to 20pp of GDP. 
What differs between the two markets is the debt path on which that improvement lands: deteriorating in the US, 
stable in the eurozone. The US is therefore the more dependent of the two on the AI productivity call actually being 
realized, as it needs the additional revenue to sustain its current expansionary fiscal stance, whereas the eurozone 
would maintain its debt trajectory even without the AI dividend.  
On our calibration, Europe’s fiscal-collateral beta is 1.6× the US per point of growth delivered, notwithstanding the 
more favorable US elasticities (higher revenue elasticity, lower spending elasticity). The difference lies in the 
denominator: a smaller debt stock (EUR1 4tn against USD 40tn), a lower starting yield, a lower baseline real rate

and a flat baseline debt path against a deteriorating one, with the CBO projecting US debt rising 20pp of GDP by

advantage would widen further (Figure 6).

The neutral rate: bigger in the US, more decisive in Europe 
The r* channel is larger in the US but more decisive in Europe. In the US upside it is the biggest single positive 
contribution at both moments, + 67bp at the 2032 peak and +42bp at the 2036 end point, but it sits inside 158bp of 
gross channel activity and is therefore largely netted away by the other channels. In the eurozone it is effectively 
the only positive channel left at the end of the period, at +15bp. Both paths rise with a delay, starting two to three 
years from now. The US path runs much higher but also mean -reverts more strongly later on, so the gap to the 
eurozone, which follows a smoother path, narrows over time. In the downside the paths are less pronounced and 
the two regions are closer together: the absolute impact is stronger in the US, while the eurozone shows the higher 
sensitivity relative to its own starting point.

AI issuance is affecting the US treasury market, the euro market is untouched for now 
The micro channels are a US story: the US dominates AI-related debt issuance and duration supply. In the eurozone 
the effect is still small, with EUR-denominated AI issuance accounting for around 1% of the corporate benchmark

The market prices the AI productivity gain, but not the fiscal relief that should come with it 
In the US, the rates market has already fully priced the productivity leg of the AI story. The 5y1y forward swap has 
climbed from 3.0% in mid-2025 to 4.9%, against a median neutral-rate estimate of 3.4% and range of 2.7-4.0%, which 
puts the market -implied neutral rate some 1 50bp above the median and 90bp above the top of the band. Our AI 
models would add +67bp to current estimates pushing the range to 3.3-4.67% – a range the market already exceeds. 
What is missing from the current A I narrative pricing is the other side of the same story: the fiscal collateral 
improvement that these productivity gains should bring, worth −85bp on the US 10Y  over the next ten years. That 
leg arrives with a much longer lag, as it takes a visible turn in tax receipts to shift the narrative (Figure 9). We would 
therefore read the current level as a temporary mispricing rather than a challenge to the fiscal leg of the AI story. 
The front-loading of the productivity channel builds up correction potential.
